Fearless Asynchronous Communications with Timed Multiparty Session Protocols
Ping Hou, Nicolas Lagaillardie, Nobuko Yoshida

Abstract
Session types using affinity and exception handling mechanisms have been developed to ensure the communication safety of protocols implemented in concurrent and distributed programming languages. Nevertheless, current affine session types are inadequate for specifying real-world asynchronous protocols, as they are usually imposed by time constraints which enable timeout exceptions to prevent indefinite blocking while awaiting valid messages. This paper proposes the first formal integration of affinity , time constraints , timeouts , and time-failure handling based on multiparty session types for supporting reliability in asynchronous distributed systems. With this theory, we statically guarantee that asynchronous timed communication is deadlock-free, communication safe, while being fearless – never hindered by timeout errors or abrupt terminations. To implement our theory, we introduce MultiCrusty T \texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T} ​, a Rust toolchain designed to facilitate the implementation of safe affine timed protocols. MultiCrusty T \texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T} ​ leverages generic types and the time library to handle timed communications, integrated with optional types for affinity. We evaluate our approach by extending diverse examples from the literature to incorporate time and timeouts, demonstrating that our solution incurs negligible overhead compared with an untimed implementation. We also showcase the correctness by construction of our approach by implementing various real-world use cases, including a remote data protocol from the Internet of Remote Things domain, as well as protocols from real-time systems like Android motion sensors and smartwatches.

1
Introduction

Background

The growing prevalence of distributed programming has emphasised the significance
of prioritising
reliability

in distributed systems.
Dedicated research efforts focus on enhancing reliability through the study and modelling of failures.
This research enables the design of more resilient distributed systems,
capable of effectively handling failures
and ensuring reliable operation.

A lightweight, type-based methodology, which
ensures basic reliability –
safety

in distributed communication systems, is

session types

[
20
]
.
This type discipline is further advanced by

Multiparty Session Types
(
MPST
)
[
21
,
22
]
,
which enable the specification and verification of communication protocols
among multiple message-passing processes in concurrent and distributed systems.

MPST
ensure that
protocols are designed
to prevent common safety errors,
i.e. deadlocks and communication mismatches during interactions among many
participants
[
21
,
22
,
43
]
.
By adhering to a specified
MPST
protocol,
participants (a.k.a. end-point programs) can communicate reliably and efficiently.
From a practical perspective,
MPST
have been implemented in
various programming
languages
[
7
,
30
,
32
,
36
,
47
,
50
]
,
facilitating their applications and providing
safety guarantees in
real-world programs.

Nevertheless,
tackling the challenges of unreliability and failures remains
a significant issue for session types.
Most session type systems operate under the assumption of flawless
and reliable communication without failures.
To address this limitation, recent works
[
35
,
18
,
19
,
32
]

have developed
affine session types

by incorporating the
affinity
mechanism that explicitly accounts for and handles unreliability
and failures within session type systems.
Unlike linear types that must be used
exactly
once, affine types can be used
at most
once,
enabling the safe dropping of subsequent types and the premature termination
of a session in the presence of protocol execution errors.

In most real-life devices and platforms, communications are predominantly asynchronous:
inner tasks and message transfers may take time. When dealing with such communications,
it becomes crucial to incorporate
time constraints
and implement
timeout failure handling

for each operation. This is necessary to avoid potential blockages where a process might wait indefinitely
for a message from a non-failed process. While various works, as explained later, address time conditions and timeouts in session types,
it is surprising that none of the aforementioned works on affine session types
tackles the handling of timeout failures during protocol execution.

This Paper

introduces a new framework,
affine timed multiparty session types
(
ATMP
),
to address the challenges of timeouts, disconnections
and other failures in asynchronous communications:

(1)

We propose
ATMP
, an extension of asynchronous
MPST
that incorporates time specifications,
affinity, and mechanisms for handling exceptions, thus facilitating effective management of failures, with a particular focus on timeouts.
Additionally, we
demonstrate that properties from
MPST
,
i.e.
type safety
,
protocol conformance
,
and
deadlock-freedom
, are guaranteed for well-typed processes, even
in the presence of timeouts and their corresponding handling mechanism;

(2)

We present
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
our
Rust
toolchain designed for building asynchronous timed multiparty protocols under
ATMP
:

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ enables the implementation of protocols adhering to the properties of
ATMP
.

The primary focus of
ATMP
lies in
effectively
handling
timeouts during process execution,
in contrast to the approaches in
[
6
,
5
]
,
which aim to completely
avoid
time failures. Bocchi et al.
[
6
]
introduce
time conditions in
MPST
to ensure precise timing in communication protocols,
while their subsequent work
[
5
]
extends
binary
timed session types
to incorporate timeouts, allowing for more robust handling of time constraints. Yet, they
adopt strict requirements to
prevent
timeouts.
In
[
6
]
,
feasibility
and
wait-freedom

are required in their protocol design.
Feasibility requires precise time specifications for protocol termination,
while wait-freedom prohibits overlapping time windows for senders and receivers in a protocol,
which is not practical in real-world applications. Similarly, in
[
5
]
, strong conditions including
progress

of an entire set of processes
and
urgent receive
are imposed.
The progress property is usually
undecidable
,
and the urgent receive condition,
which demands immediate message reception upon availability,
is infeasible with asynchronous communication.

Recently,

[
34
]
proposes the inclusion of timeout as the unique failure notation in
MPST
,
offering flexibility in handling failures.
Time also plays a role in
synchronous
communication systems, where
[
25
]
develops
rate
-based
binary
session types,
ensuring
synchronous
message exchanges at the same
rate
, i.e. within the same time window.
However, in both
[
34
]
and
[
25
]
,
time constraints are not integrated into types and static type checking,
resulting in the specifications lacking the ability to guide time behaviour.
Additionally,
the model used in
[
25
]
assumes that all communications and computations are non-time-consuming, i.e. with zero time cost, making it unfeasible in distributed systems.

By the efficient integration of time and failure handling mechanisms in our framework,
none of those impractical requirements outlined in
[
6
,
5
]

is necessary. In
ATMP
, when a process encounters a timeout error,
a mechanism for handling time failures is triggered,
notifying all participants about the timeout,
leading to the termination of those participants and ultimately ending the session.
Such an approach guarantees that participants consistently
reach the
end of the protocol
,
as the communication session is entirely dropped
upon encountering a timeout error.
As a result,
every process can terminate successfully,
reducing the risk of indefinite blockages, even with timeouts.
Additionally, in our system, time constraints over local clocks are incorporated with types to effectively model asynchronous timed communication, addressing the limitations in
[
34
,
25
]
.

Except for
[
25
]
,
the aforementioned works on timed session types focus more on theory,
lacking implementations.
To bridge this gap on the practical side,
we provide
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
a
Rust
implementation of
ATMP
designed for secure timed communications.

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ makes use of affine timed meshed channels,
a communication data structure that integrates time constraints and clock utilisation.
Our toolchain relies on macros and native generic types
to ensure that asynchronous protocols are inherently
correct by construction
.
In particular,

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ performs compile-time verification to guarantee that,
at any given point in the protocol,
each isolated pair of participants comprises one sender and one receiver
with corresponding time constraints.
Additionally,
we employ affine asynchronous primitives and native optional types
to effectively handle
runtime
timeouts and errors.

To showcase the capabilities and expressiveness of our toolchain,
we evaluate
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ through examples from the literature, and further
case studies including a remote data protocol from an Internet of Remote Things (IoRT) network
[
9
]
,
a servo web protocol from a web engine
[
44
]
, and protocols from real-time systems such as Android motion sensor
[
2
]
, PineTime smartwatch
[
39
]
, and keyless entry
[
48
]
.
Our comparative analysis with
a
Rust
implementation of affine
MPST
without time
[
32
]

reveals that
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ exhibits minimal overhead
while providing significantly strengthened property checks.

Contributions and Structure

§​

2
offers a comprehensive overview of our theory and toolchain,
employing the
remote data

as a running example.

§​

3
provides a session
\pi
-calculus for
ATMP
that incorporates timeout, affinity, asynchrony, and failure handling mechanisms.

§​

4

introduces an extended theory of asynchronous multiparty session types
with time annotations, elucidating the behavioural correspondence between timed types
in
Thms.

15
and
14
.
Additionally,
we present a typing system for
ATMP
session

\pi
-calculus, and demonstrate the properties of typed processes, including subject reduction (processes preserve well-typedness), session fidelity (processes adhere to their types),
and deadlock-freedom (processes never get stuck),
in
Thms.

21
,
25
and
28
.
§​

5

delves into the design and usage of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
our
Rust
implementation of
ATMP
.

§​

6
showcases
the compilation and execution benchmarks of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
based on selected case studies.
Finally,
§​

7
concludes the paper
by discussing related work, and offering conclusions and potential future work. Full proofs, auxiliary material,
and more details of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ can be found in Appendix.
Additionally, our toolchain and evaluation examples are available in an artifact,
which can be accessed on
Zenodo
.
For those interested in the source files, they are available
on
GitHub
.

2
Overview

In this section, we give an overview of affine timed multiparty session types (
ATMP
) and
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, our toolchain for implementing affine timed asynchronous protocols.
First,
we share
a real-world example inspiring our work on
affine asynchronous timed communication.

Fig.

1(b)
depicts
our running example,
remote data
.
This real-world scenario is sourced from a satellite-enabled Internet of Remote
Things network
[
9
]
, and describes
data transmissions among a
Sensor
(
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
),
a
Server
(
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
), and a
Satellite
(
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
):
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
aims to periodically retrieve
data gathered by
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}

via
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
.
The protocol revolves around a loop initiated by
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
,
which faces a decision:
either retrieve data or end the protocol.
In the former scenario,

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
requests data retrieval
from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
with a message labelled
GetData
via
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}

within the time window of
5
and
6
time units, as indicated by
clock constraints (i.e.
5\leq C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}\leq 6
,
where
C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
is the clock associated with
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
).
Upon receiving this request,

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
responds by sending the data with
a message labelled
Data

to
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
through
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
within

6
and
7
time units, followed by clock resets denoted
as reset predicates (i.e.
C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}:=0
, resetting the clock
to
0
).
In the alternative branch,

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
sends a
Close
message to

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
, which is then forwarded to
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
,
between
5
and
6
time units.

Our remote data protocol
includes internal tasks that consume time,
notably
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
requiring
5
time units to gather data before transmitting.
In cases where our protocol lacks a specified timing strategy (i.e. no time requirements), and
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
cannot accomplish the data-gathering tasks, it results in indefinite blocking for
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}

and
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
as they await the data.
This could lead to undesirable outcomes, including partially processed data, data corruption, or incomplete transmission of processed data.
Therefore, incorporating time constraints into communication protocols is imperative, as it better reflects real-world scenarios and ensures practical viability.

2.1
ATMP
: Theory Overview

\nu

​

with
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}

Thms
14
,
15

(a)

Top-down view of
ATMP
(left) and
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ (right).

(b)

Global protocol for
remote data
.

Figure 1
:
Overview of affine asynchronous communication with time.

Our
ATMP
theory follows the
top-down
methodology
[
21
,
22
]
,
enhancing asynchronous MPST with time features to facilitate timed global and local types.
As shown in
Fig.

1(a)
(left),
we specify multiparty
protocols with time as
timed global types
.
These timed global types are projected into
timed local types
, which are
then used for type-checking processes
with affine types, time, timeouts, and failure handling, written in a session calculus. As an example, we consider a simple communication
scenario derived from remote data:
the Satellite (
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
)
communicates with the server (
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
)
by sending a
Data
message (
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{Data}}
).
Specifically,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
needs to send the message between 6 and 7
time units and reset its clock afterwards, while
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
is expected to
receive the message within the same time window and reset its clock accordingly.

Timed Types and Processes

This communication behaviour
can be represented by the timed global type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
:

\small{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}{\to}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{Data}}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}6\leq C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}\leq 7,C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}:=0,6\leq C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}\leq 7,C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}:=0}\}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}}}}\right\}}}

where
C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
and
C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
denote the clocks of

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
, respectively.
A global type represents a protocol specification involving multiple roles from a global standpoint.

Adhering to the MPST top-down approach,
a timed global type is then
projected
onto timed local
types, which describe communications from the perspective of individual roles.
In our example,
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
is projected onto two timed local types,
one for each role
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
:

\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}=\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{Data}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}6\leq C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}\leq 7,C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}:=0}\}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}=\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{Data}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}6\leq C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}\leq 7,C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}:=0}\}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}

Here
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
indicates
that
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
sends (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}
) the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{Data}}

to
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}

between 6 and 7 time units and then immediately resets its clock
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
.
Dually,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
denotes
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}

receiving (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}
) the message from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}

within the same time frame
and resetting its clock
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
.

In the final step of the top-down approach, we employ timed local types to conduct type-checking for processes, denoted as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}
, in the
ATMP
session calculus. Our session calculus extends the framework for
affine multiparty session types
(
AMPST
)
[
32
]

by incorporating processes that model time, timeouts, and asynchrony.
In our example,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
are used for the type-checking of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}]}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}
, which respectively represent
the channels (a.k.a. session endpoints) played by roles
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
in a multiparty session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, within the processes:

\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({C_{1}=6.5})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}]}}^{0.4}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Data}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}}}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({C_{2}=6})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}}^{0.3}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}}]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Data}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}}}}}}

The Satellite process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
waits for exactly

6.5
time units (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}(C_{1}=6.5)}
),
then sends the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Data}}
with a timeout of
0.4
time units (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}]}}^{0.4}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Data}}}}}}
),
and becomes inactive (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
).
Meanwhile,
the Server process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}

waits for
6
time units (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}(C_{2}=6)}
), then receives the message
with a timeout of
0.3
time units (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}}^{0.3}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}}]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Data}}}}}}
), subsequently becoming inactive.

Solution to Stuck Processes Due to Time Failures

It appears that the parallel execution of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
,
cannot proceed further due to the disparity in timing requirements.
Specifically, using the same session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}

sends the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Data}}
to
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}

between
6.5
and
6.9
time units,
while
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
must
receive it from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
between
6
and
6.3
time units.
This results in a stuck situation, as
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
cannot meet the required timing condition to receive the message.

Fortunately, in our system, timeout failures are allowed, which can be addressed by leveraging affine session types and
their associated failure handling mechanisms. Back to our example, when
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}
waits for
6
time units and cannot receive
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Data}}
within
0.3
time units, a timeout failure is raised (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{timeout}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}}^{0.3}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}}]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Data}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}}}}]
).
Furthermore, we apply our time-failure handling approach to manage this timeout failure, initiating the termination of
the channel
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}
and triggering the cancellation process of the session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
(
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning
)
.
As a result, the process will successfully terminate by canceling (or killing) all usages of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
within it.

Conversely, the system introduced in
[
6
]
enforces strict requirements, including
feasibility
and
wait-freedom
, on timed global types
to prevent time-related failures in well-typed processes,
thus preventing them from becoming blocked due to unsolvable timing constraints.
Feasibility ensures the successful termination of each allowed partial execution,
while wait-freedom guarantees that receivers do not have to wait if senders follow their time constraints. In our example, we start with a timed global type that is neither feasible nor wait-free,
showcasing how our system effectively handles time failures
and ensures successful process termination
without imposing additional conditions on timed global types.
In essence,
reliance on feasibility and wait-freedom becomes
unnecessary in our system, thanks to the inclusion of affinity
and time-failure handling mechanisms.

2.2
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​: Toolchain Overview

⬇

1

struct

Send
<
T
,

2

,

:

,

:

,

type

,

:

,

,

:

,

,

,

:

,

type

⬇

1

MeshedChannels
<

2

Recv
<
Data
,

3

’
a
’,6,
true
,7,
true
,’
a
’,
End
>,

4

Send
<
Data
,

5

’

’,6,

,7,

,’

’,

>,

<

,

(c)

MeshedChannels
type for
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}

Figure 2
:
Main types of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​

To augment the theory,
we introduce the
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ library,
a toolchain for implementing communication protocols in
Rust
.

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ specifies protocols where communication operations
must adhere to specific time limits (
timed
),
allowing for
asynchronous
message reception and
runtime handling of certain failures (
affine
).
This library relies on two fundamental types:
Send
and
Recv
,
representing message sending and receiving, respectively.
Additionally, it incorporates the
End
type, signifying termination to close the connection.

Figs.

2(a)
and
2(b)

illustrate the
Send
and
Recv
types respectively,
used for sending and receiving messages of any thread-safe type
(represented as
T
in
1
).
After sending or receiving a message,
the next operation or continuation
(
S
in
8
) is determined,
which may entail sending another message,
receiving another message,
or terminating the connection.

Similar to
ATMP
,
each communication operation in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ is constrained by specific time boundaries to avoid infinite waiting.
These time bounds are represented by the parameters in

2
to
7

of
Fig.

2(a)
,
addressing scenarios where a role may be required to send
after a certain time unit or receive between two specific time units.
Consider the final communication operation in the first branch
of
Fig.

1(b)
from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
’s perspective.
To remain consistent with
§​

2.1
,
the communication is terminated here instead of looping
back to the beginning of the protocol.
In this operation,

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
sends a message labelled
Data

to
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
between time units
6
and
7
,
with respect to its inner clock
’
b
’
,
and then terminates after resetting its clock.
This can be implemented as:
Send
<
Data
,

’
b
’,

6,

true
,

7,

true
,

’
b
’,

End
>
.

To enable multiparty communication in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, we
use the
MeshedChannels
type, inspired by
[
32
]
.
This choice is necessary as
Send
and
Recv
types are primarily
designed for
binary
(peer-to-peer) communication.
Within
MeshedChannels
, each binary channel pairs the owner role with another,
establishing a mesh of communication channels that encompasses all participants.

Fig.

2(c)
demonstrates
an example of using
MeshedChannels

for
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
in our running example:

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
receives a
Data
message from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}

(
2
) and forwards it to
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
(
4
)
before ending all communications,
following the order specified by the stack in
6
.

Creating these types manually in
Rust
can be challenging and error-prone,
especially because they represent the local perspective of each role in the protocol.
Therefore, as depicted in
Fig.

1(a)
(right),

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ employs a top-down methodology similar to
ATMP
to
generate local viewpoints from a global protocol,
while ensuring the
correctness
of the generated types
by construction
.
To achieve this, we extend the syntax of
\nu
Scr

[
50
]
,
a language for describing multiparty communication protocols,
to include time constraints, resulting in
\nu
Scr
T
.
A timed global protocol represented in
\nu
Scr
T
is then projected onto local types,
which are used for generating
Rust
types in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​.

3
Affine Timed Multiparty Session Calculus

In this section, we formalise an affine timed multiparty session
\pi
-calculus,
where processes are capable of performing time actions, raising timeouts, and handling failures.
We start with the formal definitions of time constraints used in the paper.

Clock Constraint, Valuation, and Reset

Our time model is based on the timed automata
formalism
[
1
,
31
]
.
Let
\mathbf{C}
denote a finite set of
clocks
, ranging over
C,C^{\prime},C_{1},\ldots
,
that take non-negative real values in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Additionally, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t_{1}},\ldots
be
time constants
ranging over
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
A
clock constraint

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
over
\mathbf{C}
is defined as:

\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\,\Coloneqq\,\texttt{true}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}C>\mathfrak{b}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}C=\mathfrak{b}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}_{1}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}_{2}

where
C\in\mathbf{C}
and
\mathfrak{b}
is a
constant time bound
ranging over non-negative rationals

\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
. We define
\texttt{false},<,\geq,\leq
in the standard way.
For simplicity and consistency with our implementation (
§​

5
),
we assume each clock constraint contains a
single
clock.
Extending a clock constraint with
multiple
clocks is straightforward.

A
clock valuation

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}:\mathbf{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

assigns time to each clock in
\mathbf{C}
.
We define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}+t
as the valuation that assigns to each
C\in\mathbf{C}
the value
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}(C)+t
.
The
initial
valuation that maps all clocks to
0
is denoted as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}^{0}
,
and the valuation that assigns a value of
t
to all clocks is denoted as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}^{t}
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
indicates that the constraint

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
is satisfied by the valuation

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}
.
Additionally,
we use
\sqcup_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}_{i}}
to represent the overriding union of the valuations
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}_{i}}
for
i\in I
.

A
reset predicate

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda}
over
\mathbf{C}

is a subset of
\mathbf{C}
that defines the clocks to be reset.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda}=\emptyset
, no reset is performed.
Otherwise,
the valuation for each clock
C\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda}
is set to
0
.
For clarity,
we represent a reset predicate as
C:=0

when a single clock
C
needs to be reset.
To denote the clock valuation identical to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}

but with the values of clocks in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda}
to
0
,
we use
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}{}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda}\mapsto 0]
.

Syntax of Processes

Our session
\pi
-calculus for affine timed multiparty session types (
ATMP
)
models timed processes interacting
via affine meshed multiparty channels.
It extends the calculus for affine multiparty session types (
AMPST
)
[
32
]

by incorporating asynchronous communication,
time features, timeouts, and failure handling.
1
1

1

To simplify,
our calculus exclusively emphasises send/receive communication channels.
Standard extensions, e.g. integers, booleans, and conditionals,
can easily integrate into our framework since they are independent of our main formulation;
they will be used in the examples.

Definition 1

(Syntax)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}},\ldots
denote
roles
belonging to a (fixed) set
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
;

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}},\ldots
for
sessions
;

x,y,\ldots
for
variables
;

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}},\ldots
for
message labels
; and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y},\ldots
for
process variables
.
The
affine timed multiparty session

\pi
-calculus
syntax is defined as follows:

\small\begin{array}[]{r@{\hskip 2mm}c@{\hskip 2mm}l@{\hskip 2mm}l}\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}\hskip 5.69054pt&\Coloneqq\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(variable, channel with role ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$)}\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\hskip 5.69054pt&\Coloneqq\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(inaction, parallel composition, restriction)}\\
\hskip 5.69054pt&\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}$}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(timed selection towards role ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}$)}\\
\hskip 5.69054pt&\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]}\mathbin{\!\sum_{i\in I}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}}({x_{i}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}}}}{}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]}\mathbin{\!\sum_{i\in I}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}}({x_{i}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}}}}{}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]}\mathbin{\!\sum_{i\in I}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}}({x_{i}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}}}}{}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]}\mathbin{\!\sum_{i\in I}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}}({x_{i}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}}}}{}$}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(timed branching from role ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}$ with $I\neq\emptyset$)}\\
\hskip 5.69054pt&\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{def}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}D}}\;\mathbf{in}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\!\left\langle{\widetilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}}\right\rangle}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(process definition, process call)}\\
\hskip 5.69054pt&\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\emph{\mbox{$\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}$}}$}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\emph{\mbox{\textul{$\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}$}}$}}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(time-consuming delay, \textul{deterministic delay})}\\
\hskip 5.69054pt&\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\textul{$\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{timeout}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}]$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{timeout}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}]$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{timeout}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}]$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{timeout}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}]$}}$}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\emph{\mbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{try}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\ \operatorname{\text{{catch}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}$}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(\textul{timeout failure}, try-catch)}\\
\hskip 5.69054pt&\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\emph{\mbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{cancel}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}$}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{cerr}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\mbox{\textul{${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning$}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(cancel, communication error, \textul{kill})}\\
\hskip 5.69054pt&\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}$}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(output message queue of role ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$ in session ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}$)}\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}D}\hskip 5.69054pt&\Coloneqq\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}(\widetilde{x})={P}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(declaration of process variable ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}$)}\\[2.84526pt]
\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}$}}\hskip 5.69054pt&\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}]}}\rangle}}\mathrel{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}]}}\rangle}}\mathrel{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}]}}\rangle}}\mathrel{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}]}}\rangle}}\mathrel{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}$}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}$}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\mbox{\small(message queue, non-empty or empty)}\end{array}

Restriction, branching, and process definitions and declarations act as binders;

\operatorname{fc}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\right)
is the set of
free channels with roles
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
,
\operatorname{fv}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\right)
is
the set of
free variables
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
, and
\Pi_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}

is the parallel composition of processes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}
.
Extensions w.r.t.
AMPST

calculus are
highlighted
.
Runtime processes, generated dynamically during program execution rather than explicitly written by users,
are
underlined
.

Our calculus comprises:

Channels

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}
, being either variables
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}

or channels with roles (a.k.a. session
endpoints
)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}
.

Standard
processes as in
[
43
,
32
]
, including
inaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
, parallel composition
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
, session scope restriction

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, process definition
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{def}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}D}}\;\mathbf{in}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, process
call
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\!\left\langle{\widetilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}}\right\rangle}
, and communication error
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{cerr}}}
.

Time
processes that follow the program time behaviour of
Fig.

2(c)
:

•

Timed selection
(or
timed internal choice
)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}

indicates that a message

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}
with payload
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}
is sent to role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
via endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}
,
whereas
timed branching
(or
timed external choice
)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]}\mathbin{\!\sum_{i\in I}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}}({x_{i}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}}}}{}
waits to
receive a message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}
from role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
via endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}
and then proceeds as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}
.

The parameter
\mathfrak{n}
in both timed selection and branching is a
timeout
that allows modelling
different types of communication primitives:
blocking with a timeout

(
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
),
blocking
(
\mathfrak{n}=\infty
), or
non-blocking
(
\mathfrak{n}=0
).
When
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the timed selection (or timed branching) process waits for up to
\mathfrak{n}
time units to send (or receive) a message.
If the message cannot be sent (or received) within this time, the process moves into a
timeout state
, raising a
time failure
.
If
\mathfrak{n}
is set to
\infty
, the timed selection (or timed branching) process blocks until a message is successfully sent (or received).

In our system, we allow
send
processes to be time-consuming, enabling processes to wait before sending messages.
Consider the remote data example shown in
Fig.

1(b)
.
This practical scenario illustrates how a process might wait before sending a message, resulting in the possibility of send actions failing due to timeouts. It highlights the importance of timed selection, contrasting with systems like in
[
5
]
where send actions are instantaneous.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
represents a
time-consuming delay
action, such as method invocation or sleep.
Here,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
is a clock constraint involving a
single
clock variable
C
, used to specify the interval for the delay.
When executing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, any time value
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}
that satisfies the constraint
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
can be consumed.
Consequently, the
runtime

deterministic delay
process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, arising during the execution of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, is introduced. In
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}
is a constant and a solution to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is executed after a precise delay of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}
time units.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{timeout}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}]
signifies that the process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
has violated a time constraint, resulting in a

timeout failure
.

Failure-handling
processes that adopt the
AMPST
approach
[
32
]
:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{try}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\ \operatorname{\text{{catch}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}

consists of a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{try}}}}
process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
that is prepared to communicate
with a parallel composed process,
and a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{catch}}}}
process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
, which becomes active in the event of a cancellation or timeout.
For clarity,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{try}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}}\ \operatorname{\text{{catch}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}
is not allowed within our calculus.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{cancel}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
performs the
cancellation
of other processes with channel
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}
.

•

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning

kills
(terminates) all processes with session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
,
and is dynamically generated only at
runtime
from timeout failure or cancel processes.

Message queues
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}

represents the
output message queue
of role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
.
It contains all the messages previously sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
. The queue
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}
can be a sequence of messages of the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}]}}\rangle}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
is the receiver,
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}
, indicating an
empty
message queue.
The set of
receivers in

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}
, denoted as
\operatorname{receivers}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma})
,
is defined in a standard way as:

\small\operatorname{receivers}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}]}}\rangle}}\mathrel{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma^{\prime}}}})=\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}\right\}}}\cup\operatorname{receivers}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma^{\prime}})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{receivers}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon})=\emptyset

Figure 4
:

Time-passing function
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
