Figure 9
:
ATMP
typing rules.

The innovations (
highlighted
) in
Fig.

9
primarily
focus on typing processes with time, timeout failures, message queues, and using association (
Def.

12
) to enforce session restrictions.

Standard
from
[
43
]
: Rule
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
]
retrieves process variables.
Rule
[T-Sub]
applies subtyping within a singleton typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}})}}}
.
Rule
[T-
\operatorname{end}
]
introduces a predicate
\operatorname{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\cdot})
for typing environments,
signifying the termination of all endpoints. This predicate is used in
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
]
to type an inactive process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
. Rules
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{def}}
]
and

[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
-Call]
deal with recursive processes
declarations and calls, respectively.
Rule
[T-
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}
]
partitions the typing environment into two,
each dedicated to typing one sub-process.

Session Restriction
: Rule
[T-
\nu
-
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
]
depends on a typing environment associated with a timed global type in a given session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
to validate session restrictions.

Delay
: Rule
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
]
ensures the typedness of time-consuming delay

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
by checking every deterministic delay
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({t})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
with
t
as a possible solution to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
. Rule
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}
]
types a deterministic delay
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({t})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
by adjusting the clock valuations in the environment used to type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
. Here,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}+t
denotes the typing environment obtained from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
by increasing the associated clock valuation in each entry by
t
.

Timed Branching and Selection
: Rules
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\&}
]
and
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oplus}
]
are for timed branching and selection, respectively. We elaborate on
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\&}
]
, as
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oplus}
]
is its dual.
The first premise in
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\&}
]

specifies a time interval
[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}+\mathfrak{n}]
within which the message must be received,
in accordance with each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta_{i}}
.
The last premise requires that each continuation process be well-typed against the continuation of the type in all possible typing environments
where the time falls between
[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}+\mathfrak{n}]
.
Here, the clock valuation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}
is reset based on each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda_{i}}
.
The remaining premises stipulate that the clock valuation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}_{i}}
of each delegated receiving session must satisfy
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{i}}
, and that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}
is typed.

Try-Catch, Cancellation, and Kill
:
Rules
[T-Try]
,
[T-Cancel]
, and
[T-Kill]
pertain to try-catch, cancellation, and kill processes, respectively, analogous to the corresponding rules in
[
32
]
.

[T-Cancel]
is responsible for generating a kill process at its declared session.

[T-Kill]
types a kill process arising during reductions: it involves broadcasting the cancellation of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}
to all processes that belong to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
.

[T-Try]
handles a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{try-catch}}}}
process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{try}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\ \operatorname{\text{{catch}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}

by ensuring that
the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{try}}}}
process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
and the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{catch}}}}
process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}

maintain consistent session typing. Additionally,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
cannot be a queue or parallel composition, as indicated by

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{subjP}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}})}=\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}\right\}}}
.

Timeout Failure
: Rule
[T-Failed]
indicates that a process raising timeout failure
can be typed by
any
typing environment.

Queue
:
Rules
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}
]
and
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}
]
concern the typing of queues.
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}
]
types an empty queue
under an ended typing environment, while
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}
]
types a non-empty queue by inserting a message type into
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
. This insertion may either prepend the message to an existing queue type in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
or add a queue-typed entry to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
if not present.

Example 17
.

Take the typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}
from
Ex.

13
, along with the processes

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
from
Ex.

5
.
Verifying the typing of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}
is easy.
Moreover,
since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}
is associated with a timed global type

\langle{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}=0,C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}=0,C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}=0\right\}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{data}}}}\rangle
for session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
(as demonstrated in
Ex.

13
),
i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}=0,C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}=0,C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}=0\right\}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{data}}}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}}}
,
following
[T-
\nu
-
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
]
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
is closed under

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}
, i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}}}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}}}}}
.

Note that the typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{\text{data}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\text{data}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\text{data}}}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\text{data}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\text{data}}}}}
(
Ex.

13
), and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\text{data}}}}}
is obtained from projecting

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{data}}}
, i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\text{data}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}=0\right\}}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{data}}}}{\upharpoonright}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}}}})}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}=0\right\}}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{data}}}}{\upharpoonright}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}}}})}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}=0\right\}}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{data}}}}{\upharpoonright}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}}}})}}}
,
is capable of typing the process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
as well. In fact, if a process is typable by a typing environment associated with a timed global type, then it can definitely be typed using the environment derived from the projection of the timed global type, highlighting the utility of association.

4.5
Typed Process Properties

We demonstrate that processes typed by the
ATMP
typing system exhibit the desirable properties:

subject reduction
(
Thm.

21
),

session fidelity
(
Thm.

25
),
and
deadlock-freedom
(
Thm.

28
).

Subject Reduction

ensures the preservation of well-typedness of processes during reductions.
Specifically, it states that if a well-typed process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
reduces to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
, this reduction is reflected in the typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
used to type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
. Notably, in our subject
reduction theorem,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is constructed from a timed global type, i.e. typed by an environment
associated with a timed global type, and this construction approach persists as an invariant property throughout reductions.
Furthermore, the theorem does not require
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
to contain only a single session; instead,
it includes all restricted sessions in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
, ensuring that reductions on these
sessions uphold their respective restrictions.
This enforcement is facilitated by rule
[T-
\nu
-
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
]
in
Fig.

9
.

Association (
Def.

12
), crucial for subject reduction, possesses the following properties that facilitate its proof: supertyping and reductions commute under association (
Lemma

18
), and supertyping preserves association (
Lemma

19
).
Their proofs are available in
Appendix §​

B
.

Lemma 18
.

Assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}
.
Then, there is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}
.

Lemma 19
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}}}
.

Additionally, the typability of time passing,
fundamental for demonstrating both subject reduction and session fidelity (as discussed later), is addressed below.
Its proof is available in
Appendix §​

D
.

Lemma 20
.

Assume

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
.
If

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rightharpoonup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}

with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}

defined, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}}}}
.

Theorem 21

(Subject Reduction)
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}

where
\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}:\exists\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}}}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
, then

\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}

such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}{}^{\!\!\!*}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
, and
\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}:\exists\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
(see
Appendix §​

D
).
∎

Corollary 22

(Type Safety)
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\emptyset}\cdot{\emptyset}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
has no communication error.

Example 23
.

Take the typed process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}

and the typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}
from
Exs.

5
,
13
and
17
.
After a reduction using
[R-Det]
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
transitions to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({6.5})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({6})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({6})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{2}}
, which remains typable by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}{}^{\!\!\!*}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}
).
Then, applying
[R-Time]
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{2}}
evolves to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{6.5}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{2}})}
, typed as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}+6.5
, derived from

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\xrightarrow{6.5}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}+6.5
.
Further reduction through
[R-Fail]
leads
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{6.5}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{2}})}
to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{0.5}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{3}}
, typable by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}+6.5
. Later, via
[C-Cat]
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{3}}
reduces to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{cancel}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}]}})}{}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{0.5}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}
, which can be typed by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\text{data}}}
, obtained from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}+6.5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}{:}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}{:}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{Data}}}}}}}\cdot\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}{:}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}{:}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{Data}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\text{data}}}
.
Due to
Thm.

14
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\text{data}}}
keeps its association with some timed global type.

Session Fidelity

states the converse implication of subject reduction: if a process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is typed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
can reduce, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
can simulate at least one of the reductions performed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
– although not necessarily all such reductions, as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
over-approximates the behavior of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
.
Consequently, we can infer

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
’s behaviour from that of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
.
However, this result does not hold for certain well-typed processes,
such as those that get trapped in recursion loops like
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{def}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}(...)={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}\;\mathbf{in}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}
,
or deadlock due to interactions across multiple sessions
[
10
]
.
To address this, similarly to
[
43
]
and most session type works, we
establish session fidelity specifically for processes featuring guarded recursion
and implementing a single multiparty session as a parallel composition of one
sub-process per role. The formalisation of session fidelity is provided in
Thm.

25
,
building upon the concepts introduced in
Def.

24
.

Definition 24

(From
[
43
]
)
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
. We say that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
:

1.

has guarded definitions
if and only if
in each process definition in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{def}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}_{1}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{1}}})}}},...,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{n}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}_{n}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{n}}})}}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}\;\mathbf{in}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
, for all
i\in 1...n
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\not\leqslant}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}

implies that a call
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}\!\left\langle{...,x_{i},...}\right\rangle}
can only occur in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
as a subterm of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{x_{i}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]}\mathbin{\!\sum_{j\in J}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathnormal{j}}}}}({y_{j}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{j}}}}}{}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x_{i}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}}}}}
(i.e. after using
x_{i}
for input or output);

2.

only plays role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
​,
if and only if

(i)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
has guarded definitions;

(ii)

\operatorname{fv}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\right)=\emptyset
;

(iii)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!=\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{0}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\tau}}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\tau}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\not\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}})}
and
\operatorname{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{0}}})
;

(iv)

in all subterms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}}}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}^{\prime}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oslash}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{;\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}})}}}}}

or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}^{\prime}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}})}}}

(for some
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}
).

We say “
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
only plays role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
” if and only if
\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, and item
2
holds.

In
Def.

24
, item
1

describes guarded recursion for processes, while
item
2
specifies a process limited to playing exactly
one
role within
one
session,
preventing an ensemble of such processes from
deadlocking by waiting for each other on multiple sessions.

We proceed to present our session fidelity result, taking kill processes into account.
We denote
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\lightning
to indicate that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
consists only of a parallel composition of kill processes.
Similar to subject reduction (
Thm.

21
), our session fidelity relies on a typing environment associated with a timed global type for a specific session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
to type the process, ensuring the fulfilment of
single-session requirements (
Def.

24
) and maintaining invariance during reductions.
The proof is by induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}_{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}
(available in
Appendix §​

E
).

Theorem 25

(Session Fidelity)
.

Assume

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
,
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\lightning
,
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{0}}
,
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{0}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\lightning}}
,
and
for each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
:

(1)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
, and

(2)

either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
only plays role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
,
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.

Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}_{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}
implies

\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}},\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}_{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\lightning
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\lightning}}
, and
for each

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
:

(1)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
,
and

(2)

either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
only plays role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
,
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.

Example 26
.

Consider the processes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
from
Ex.

5
, the process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{3}}
from
Ex.

23
,
and the typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}
from
Ex.

13
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
only play
roles
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
, and
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
, respectively, in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, which can be easily verified.

As demonstrated in
Ex.

23
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{3}}
is typed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}+6.5
, satisfying all prerequisites specified in
Thm.

25
.
Consequently, given
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}+6.5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}{:}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}{:}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{Data}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{\text{data}}}
, there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{4}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\mathrel{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}{!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Data}}}}}\mathrel{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{0.5}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}
, resulting from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{3}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{4}}
via
[R-Out]
, such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{\text{data}}}
can type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{4}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{\text{data}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{4}}
fulfilling the single session requirements of session fidelity.

Deadlock-Freedom

ensures that a process can always either progress via reduction or terminate properly. In our system, where time can be infinitely reduced and session killings may occur during reductions,
deadlock-freedom implies that if a process cannot undergo any further instantaneous (communication) reductions, and if any subsequent time reduction leaves it unchanged, then it contains only inactive or
kill sub-processes.
This desirable runtime property is guaranteed by processes constructed from timed global types.
We formalise the property in
Def.

27
, and conclude, in
Thm.

28
, that a typed ensemble of processes interacting on a single session, restricted by
a typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
associated with a timed global type
\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}^{0}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle
,
is deadlock-free. The proof is available in
Appendix §​

E
.

Definition 27

(Deadlock-Free Process)
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is
deadlock-free
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
and
\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}\geq 0:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}})}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
(recall that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}(\cdot)}
is a time-passing function defined in
Fig.

4
) implies

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}\Pi_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{i}}}\lightning
.

Theorem 28

(Deadlock-Freedom)
.

Assume

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}\!}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
,
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}}}\right){\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}^{0}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}}
,
and each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
(up to
\equiv
),
or only plays
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
.
Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is deadlock-free.

Example 29
.

Considering the processes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
from
Ex.

5
, along with the typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}
and timed global type
\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}^{0}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{data}}}}\rangle
from
Ex.

13
, we observe in
Exs.

13
,
26
and
17
that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}^{0}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{data}}}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}
only play

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
, and
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, respectively, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}}}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}}}}}
. Following the reductions described in
Ex.

5
, we obtain
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}}}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}}}\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}}}}\right){{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
. Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
cannot undergo any further
meaningful
time reduction, i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning
for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}
, indicating the deadlock-freedom of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{\text{data}}}}}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}}}}
.

5
Design and Implementation of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​

In this section,
we present our toolchain,
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, an implementation of
ATMP
in
Rust
.

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ is designed with two primary goals:
correctly cascading failure notifications,
and effectively representing and enforcing time constraints.
To achieve the first goal,
we use
Rust
’s native
?
-operator along with
optional types, inspired by
[
32
]
.
For the second objective, we begin by
discussing the key challenges encountered during implementation.

Challenge 1: Representation of Time Constraints.
To handle asynchronous timed communications using
ATMP
,
we define a time window (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
in
ATMP
)
and a corresponding behaviour for each operation.
Addressing this constraint involves two subtasks:
creating and using clocks in
Rust
, and representing
all clock constraints as shown in
§​

3
.

Rust
allows the creation of virtual clocks
that rely on the CPU’s clock and provide nanosecond-level accuracy.
Additionally, it is crucial to ensure that different behaviours can involve
blocking or non-blocking communications,
pre- or post-specific time tags,
or falling within specified time bounds.

Challenge 2: Enforcement of Time Constraints.
To effectively enforce time windows,
implementing reliable and accurate clocks and using them correctly
is imperative.
This requires addressing all cases related to time
constraints properly: clocks may be considered unreliable if they skip ticks,
do not strictly increase,
or if the API for clock comparison does not yield
results quickly enough.
Enforcing time constraints in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ involves using two libraries:
the
crossbeam_channel

Rust
library
[
11
]

for
asynchronous
message sending and receiving,
and the
Rust
standard library
time

[
45
]
for
handling and comparing virtual clocks.

5.1
Time Bounds in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​

Implementing Time Bounds

To demonstrate the integration of time bounds in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
we consider the final interaction between
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
in
Fig.

1(b)
,
specifically from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
’s perspective:

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
sends a
Close
message between time units

5
and
6
(both inclusive),
following clock
C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat2}}}}
,
which is not reset afterward.

In
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
we define the
Send
type for message transmission,
incorporating various parameters to specify requirements as

Send
<[
parameter1
],[
parameter2
],...>
.
Assuming the (payload) type
Close
is defined,
sending it using the
Send
type involves initiating with

Send
<
Close
,...>
.
If we denote
C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat2}}}}
as
’
b
’
,
the clock
’
b
’
is employed for time constraints,
expressed as

Send
<
Close
,’
b
’,...>
.
Time bounds parameters in the
Send
type follow the declaration of the clock.
In this case, both bounds are integers within the time window,
resulting in the
Send
type being parameterised as

Send
<
Close
,’
b
’,0,
true
,1,
false
,...>
.
Notably,
bounds are integers due to the limitations of
Rust
’s generic type parameters.

To ensure that the clock
’
b
’
is not reset after triggering the
send
operation,
we represent this with a whitespace char value in the
Send
type:

Send
<
Close
,’
b
’,0,
true
,1,
false
,’

’,...>
.
The last parameter,
known as the
continuation
,
specifies the operation following the sending of the integer.
In this case,
closing the connection is achieved with an
End
type.
The complete sending operation is denoted as

Send
<
Close
,

’
b
’,

0,

true
,

1,

false
,

’

’,

End
>
.

Similarly,
the
Recv
type is instantiated as

Recv
<
Close
,’
b
’,0,
true
,1,
false
,’

’,
End
>
.
The inherent mirroring of
Send
and
Recv
reflects their dual compatibility.

Figs.

2(a)
and
2(b)

provide an analysis of the functioning of

Send
and
Recv
,
detailing their parameters and features.

Generic type parameters preceded by
const

within
Send
and
Recv
types also serve as values,
representing general type categories supported by
Rust
.
This type-value duality facilitates easy verification during compilation,
ensuring compatibility between communicating parties.

Enforcing Time Bounds

It is crucial to rely on dependable clocks and APIs to enforce time constraints.

Rust
’s standard library provides the time module
[
45
]
,
enabling developers to manage clocks and measure durations between events.
This library,
utilising OS API,
offers two clock types:

Instant
(monotonic but non-steady) and

SystemTime
(steady but non-monotonic).
In
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, the
Instant
type serves for
both correctly prioritising event order and implementing virtual clocks.
Virtual clocks are maintained through a dictionary (
HashMap
in
Rust
).
Initialising an empty
HashMap
is achieved with:

let

clocks

=

HashMap
::<
char
,
Instant
>::
new
()
, while
introducing a new clock is done using:

clocks
.
insert
(’
a
’,
Instant
::
now
())
,
where
’
a
’
is the clock’s name.

Table

1
details the available primitives
provided by
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ for sending and receiving payloads,
implementing branching,
or closing a connection.
Except for the
close
method,
all primitives require the specific

HashMap
of clocks to enforce time constraints.

Table 1
:

Primitives available in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​.
Let
s
be an affine meshed channel;

p
,
a payload of a given type;

clocks
,
a
HashMap
of all clocks
linked to the specific process;

I
,
a subset of all roles in the protocol
but the current role;
and
K
,
a subset of all branches.

let

s

=

s
.
send
(
p
,

clocks
)?;

If allowed by the time constraint compared to the given clock in
clocks
,
sends a payload
p
on a channel
s

and assigns the continuation
of the session (a new meshed channel)
to a new variable
s
.

let

(
p
,

s
)

=

s
.
recv
(
clocks
)?;

If allowed by the time constraint compared to the given clock in
clocks
,
receives a payload
p

on channel
s
and assigns the continuation
of the session to a new variable
s
.

s
.
close
()

Closes the channel
s

and returns a unit type.

offer!
\(
s
,

clocks
,

\{

enum
{}_{\texttt{i}}::

variant
{}_{\texttt{k}}(\texttt{e})=>\{...\}_{k\in K}

\}\)

If allowed by the time constraint compared to the given clock in
clocks
,
role
i
receives
a choice as a message label on channel
s
,
and, depending on
the label value which should
match one of the variants

variant
k
of
enum
{}_{\texttt{i}}::
,
runs the related block of code.

choose_X!
\(
s
,

clocks
,

\{

enum
{}_{\texttt{i}}::

variant
{}_{\texttt{k}}(\texttt{e})\}_{i\in I}

\}\)

For role
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{X}}}}
, if allowed by the time constraint compared to the given clock in
clocks
,
sends the chosen label,
corresponding to
variant
k

to all other roles.

Verifying Time Bounds

Our
send
and
recv
primitives
use a series of conditions to ensure the integrity of a time window.
The verification process adopts a
divide-and-conquer
strategy,
validating the left-hand side time constraint for each clock before assessing the right-hand side constraint.
The corresponding operation, whether sending or receiving a payload, is executed only after satisfying these conditions.
This approach guarantees the effective enforcement of time constraints without requiring complex solver mechanisms.

Figure 10

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}

Implementation of Server

Fig.

10
explores our
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ implementation of

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
in the remote data protocol (
Fig.

1(b)
).
Specifically, the left side of

Fig.

10

delves into the
MeshedChannels
type,
representing the behaviour
of
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
in the first branch and encapsulating various elements.
In
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, the
MeshedChannels
type incorporates
n+1
parameters,
where
n
is the count of roles in the protocol.
These parameters include the role’s name,

n-1
binary channels for interacting with other roles,
and a stack dictating the sequence of binary channel usage.
All types relevant to
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
are depicted in
Fig.

10
(left).

The alias
EndpointSerData
, as indicated in
1
, represents the
MeshedChannels
type. Binary types, defined in
2
to
4
, facilitate communication between
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
, and
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}
. When initiating communication with
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
sends a
GetData
message in
2
, receives a
Data
response, and ends communication on this binary channel. These operations use the clock
’
a
’
and adhere to time windows between
5
and
6
seconds for the first operation and between
6
and
7
seconds for the second. Clock
’
a
’
is reset only within the second operation. The order of operations is outlined in
5
, where
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
interacts twice with
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
using
RoleSat
before initiating a choice with
RoleBroadcast
.
6
designates
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
as the owner of the
MeshedChannels
type. The behaviour of all roles in each branch can be specified similarly.

The right side of
Fig.

10
illustrates the usage of
EndpointSerData
as an input type in the
Rust
function
endpoint_data_ser
. The function’s output type,
Result
<(),

Error
>
, indicates the utilization of affinity in
Rust
. In
11
, variable
’
s
’
, of type
EndpointSerData
, attempts to send a contentless message
GetData
. The
send
function can return either a value resembling
EndpointSerData
or an
Error
. If the clock’s time does not adhere to the time constraint displayed in
2
with respect to the clock
’
a
’
from the set of clocks
clocks
, an
Error
is raised. Similarly, in
12
,
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
attempts to receive a message using the same set of clocks. Both
send
and
recv
functions verify compliance with time constraints by comparing the relevant clock provided in the type for the time window and resetting the clock if necessary.

Error Handling

The error handling capabilities of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ cover various potential errors that may arise during protocol implementation and execution. These errors include the misuse of generated types and timeouts,
showcasing the flexibility of our implementation in verifying communication protocols. For instance, if Lines
11
and
12
in
Fig.

10
are swapped, the program will fail to compile because it expects a
send
primitive in
11
, as indicated by the type of
’
s
’
. Another compile-time error occurs when a payload with the wrong type is sent. For example, attempting to send a
Data
message instead of a
GetData
in
11
will result in a compilation error.
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ can also identify errors at runtime. If the content of the function
endpoint_data_ser
, spanning in
10
to
12
, is replaced with a single
Ok
(())
, the code will compile successfully. However, during runtime, the other roles will encounter failures as they consider
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
to have failed.

Timeouts are handled dynamically within
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​.
If a time-consuming task with a 10-second delay is introduced
between Lines
11

and
12
,

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
will enter a sleep state for the same duration. Consequently, the
recv
operation
in
12
will encounter a time constraint violation,
resulting in the failure and termination of
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
.
Furthermore, the absence of clock
’
a
’
in the set of clocks, where it is required for a specific primitive,
will trigger a runtime error, as the evaluation of time constraints depends on the availability of the necessary clocks.

:

.

Timed Protocol Specification

To specify timed multiparty protocols, we extend
\nu
Scr

[
50
]
, a multiparty protocol description language, with time features, resulting in
\nu
Scr
T
. Additional keywords such as
within
,
using
, and
and

resetting
are incorporated in
\nu
Scr
to support the specification of time windows, clocks, and resets, respectively. In
Fig.

11
, we illustrate the
\nu
Scr
T
protocol for remote data, showcasing the application of these enhancements.

\nu
Scr
T
ensures the accuracy of timed multiparty protocols by verifying interactions, validating time constraints, handling clock increments, and performing standard
MPST
protocol checks.

6
Evaluation: Expressiveness, Case Studies and Benchmarks

We evaluate our toolchain
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ from two perspectives:
expressivity
and
feasibility
.
In terms of expressivity, we implement protocols from the session type literature
[
23
,
37
,
17
,
27
,
24
,
40
]
,
as well as newly introduced protocols derived from real-world applications
[
9
,
44
,
2
,
39
,
48
]
.
Regarding feasibility, we compare our system to
MultiCrusty

[
32
]
, an untimed implementation of affine synchronous
MPST
, demonstrating that our tool introduces negligible
compile-time and runtime overhead in all cases, as expected.

Setup.

For benchmarking purposes, we employ
criterion

[
28
]
, configuring it to utilise bootstrap sampling
[
14
,
16
]
for each protocol. We collect samples of size 100,000 and report the average execution time with a 95% confidence interval. Additionally, we benchmark compilation time by compiling each protocol 100 times and reporting the mean. Compilation is performed using the commands
cargo

clean
and
cargo

build

[
name

of

the

file
]
. The
cargo

clean
command removes all compiled files, including dependencies, while the second command, without additional parameters, compiles the given file and its dependencies without optimising the resulting files.
The benchmarking machine configuration is a workstation with an
Intel®
Core™
i7-7700K CPU @ 4.20 GHz
with 8 threads and 32 GB of RAM,
Manjaro Linux 23.1.4,
Linux kernel 6.6.25-1,
along with the latest versions of

Rustup
(1.27.0) and the
Rust
cargo compiler (1.77.1).

6.1
Performance:
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ vs.
MultiCrusty

When comparing
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ with
MultiCrusty
,
we evaluate their performance on two standard benchmark protocols: the
ring
and
mesh
protocols.
The ring protocol involves sequentially passing a message through roles,
while the mesh protocol requires each participant to send a message to every other.
Both protocols underwent 100 iterations within a time window of 0 to 10 seconds.
Benchmark results for roles ranging from 2 to 8 are presented in
Fig.

12
(top).

In the
ring
protocol, compile-time benchmarks (
Fig.

12(b)
) indicate that
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ experiences a marginal slowdown of less than 2% with 2 roles, but achieves approximately
5% faster compilation time with 8 roles. Regarding runtime benchmarks (
Fig.

12(d)
),
MultiCrusty
demonstrates a 15% speed advantage with 2 roles, which decreases to 5% with 8 roles. The overhead remains consistent, with a difference of less than 0.5 ms at 6, 7, and 8 roles.

In the
mesh
protocol, where all roles send and receive messages (compile-time benchmarks in
Fig.

12(a)
and runtime benchmarks in
Fig.

12(c)
),
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ compiles slightly slower (less than 1% at 2 roles,
4% at 8 roles) and runs slower as well (less than 1% at 2 roles, 15% at 8 roles). Compile times for
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ range from 18.9 s to 26 s, with running times ranging between 0.9 ms and 11.9 ms. The gap widens exponentially due to the increasing number of enforced time constraints. In summary, as the number of roles increases,
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ demonstrates a growing overhead, mainly attributed to the incorporation of additional time constraint checking.

6.2
Expressivity and Feasibility with Case Studies

(a)

mesh
- compilation

(b)

ring
- compilation

(c)

mesh
- runtime

(d)

ring
- runtime

Figure 12
:

Top: microbenchmark results for mesh and ring protocols.
Bottom: benchmark results for
Calculator
[
23
]
(a),
Online wallet
[
37
]
(b),
SMTP
[
40
]
(c),
Simple voting
[
23
]
(d),
Three buyers
[
27
]
(e),
Travel agency
[
24
]
(f),
OAuth
[
37
]
(g),
HTTP
[
17
]
(h),
Remote data
[
9
]
(i),
Servo
[
44
]
(j),
Gravity sensor
[
2
]
(k),
PineTime heart rate
[
39
]
(l),
and Proximity based car key
[
48
]
(m).

We implement a variety of protocols to showcase the expressivity, feasibility, and capabilities of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, conducting benchmarking using both
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ and
MultiCrusty
. The
send
and
recv
operations in both libraries are ordered, directed, and involve the same set of participants. Additionally, when implemented with
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, these operations are enriched with time constraints and reset predicates. The benchmark results for the selected case studies, including those from prior research and five additional protocols sourced from industrial use cases
[
9
,
44
,
2
,
39
,
48
]
, are presented in the bottom part of
Fig.

12
. To ensure a fair comparison between
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ (
\textstyle\color[rgb]{0.582,0.4023,0.7422}bars
) and
MultiCrusty
(
\textstyle\color[rgb]{0.8398,0.1523,0.1563}bars
), time constraints are enforced for all examples without introducing any additional sleep or timeouts.

It is noteworthy
that rate-based protocols ((k), (l), (m) in
Fig.

12
(bottom)) from real-time systems
[
2
,
39
,
48
]

are implemented in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, showcasing its expressivity in real-time applications.
These implementations feature the establishment of consistent time constraints and a shared clock for operations with identical rates.

Consider the Car Key protocol
[
48
]
as an example: the car periodically sends a wake-up message to probe the presence of the key. The communication in this protocol can
be easily captured by session types.
The critical aspect is the rate requirement, indicating that interactions
from one wake-up to the next must be completed within a period of (e.g.) 100 ms.
Consequently, when implementing this protocol with
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, all time constraints use the same clock, ranging between 0 and 100 ms, and the clock resets at the end of each loop. For scenarios requiring a “
generalised expansion
”, where processes and communication operations with periods of one are not multiples of the other, we extend the main loop to match the least common multiple of the periods, following a methodology detailed in
[
48
]
.

The feasibility of our tool,
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​, is demonstrated in
Fig.

12
(bottom). The results indicate that
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ incurs minimal compile-time overhead, averaging approximately 1.75%. Moreover, the runtime for each protocol remains within milliseconds, ensuring negligible impact. Notably, in the case of the HTTP protocol, the runtime comparison percentage with
MultiCrusty
is 87.60%, primarily attributed to the integration of 126 time constraints within it.
The relevant implementation metrics, including multiple participants (MP), branching, recursion (Rec), and time constraints, are illustrated in
Table

2
.
