∎

Proposition 68
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\lightning
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{0}}
such that,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{0}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\lightning}}
, and for each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
. Further, assume that each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}

is either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
(up to
\equiv
), or only plays
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
. Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}

implies
\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}},\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to^{\!*}}_{\!\!\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\lightning
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}
such that,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\lightning}}
, and for each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
,
we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
; furthermore, each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}

is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
(up to
\equiv
),
or only plays
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.

Proof.

Straightforward from the proof of
Thm.

21
, which accounts for all possible transitions from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
, and in all cases yields the desired properties for its typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}
.
∎

Lemma 69
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
such that for each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
. Further, assume that each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is either

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
(up to
\equiv
), or only plays
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
. Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning
is deadlock-free.

Proof.

By
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning}}
, we have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning}}
,
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}}
.

Consider any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
and

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}\geq 0:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}})}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
.
By
Prop.

68
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
is of the form
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\lightning
, and its typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}
is of the form

\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}
such that, there exists
\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\lightning}}
, and for each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
,
we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
.
Furthermore,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
satisfies the single-session requirements of
Thm.

25
.

Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is typed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, with the association
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma^{\prime}}}}
, we have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is not a message queue. Due to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
satisfying the
single-session requirements of
Thm.

25
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{subjP}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}})}=\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}\right\}}}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
.
We are left to show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\lightning
for each
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
The proof proceeds by induction on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}
must be
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}
, as otherwise,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\rightarrowtail
, a contradiction to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}}}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}\rightarrowtail
,
a contradiction to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}^{\mathfrak{n}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]}\mathbin{\!\sum_{i\in I}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}}({x_{i}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R_{i}}}}}{}
:
either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}\rightarrowtail
, or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\rightarrowtail

a contradiction to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{def}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}D}}\;\mathbf{in}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\rightarrowtail
, a contradiction to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\rightarrowtail
, a contradiction to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}}}
: we only consider
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}>0
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{delay}}}({0})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}
. Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t^{\prime}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}})}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
for any

, a contradiction.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{timeout}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}}]
: since
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{subjP}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}})}=\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}\right\}}}
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\rightarrowtail
, a contradiction to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{try}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}}\ \operatorname{\text{{catch}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R^{\prime}}}}
: since
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{subjP}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}})}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{subjP}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}})}=\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}\right\}}}
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning\rightarrowtail
, a contradiction to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{cancel}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}})}{\ \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\rightarrowtail
, a contradiction to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
.
∎

Lemma 70
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
such that for each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
. Further, assume that each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is either

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
(up to
\equiv
), or only plays
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is deadlock-free.

Proof.

We write
\operatorname{End}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}})

if
\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\right)}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\right)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oslash}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{;\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\mathbin{\mathbf{,\,}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}})}}}
.

Consider any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\rightarrowtail}
,

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}\geq 0:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}})}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{0}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{1}}\!\to\!\cdots\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{n}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not\!\!{\to}
(for some
n
)
with each reduction
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i+1}}
(
i\!\in\!0...n\!-\!1
).
By
Prop.

68
, we know that each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}
is well-typed
and its typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{i}}
is such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to^{\!*}}_{\!\!\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{i}}
;
The proof proceeds by considering the following two cases:

•

\operatorname{End}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{n}}})
: by
Thm.

25
(session fidelity), we know that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
is typed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{n}}
.
Therefore, by inversion of typing and
\operatorname{End}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{n}}})
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathrel{\!\blacktriangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\lightning\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\lightning
, which is the thesis.

•

\operatorname{End}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{n}}})
not satisfied: there exists
i<n
such that starting from
i
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{i}}
can only undergo reduction via
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}}
with
\operatorname{End}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma_{i}}})
not satisfied. Therefore, by
Thm.

25
, some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{j}}
with
j\geq i
performs a time reduction, resulting in a timeout, and consequently
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\lightning
. Then, the thesis holds by
Lemma

69
.
∎

28

Proof.

Lemma

.

Appendix F
Extended Details for
§​

5

F.1
Implementing Time Bounds

To demonstrate the implementation of time bounds in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
we focus on the final interaction between
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
,
from the point of view of
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}
.
In this case,

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
sends a
Close
message between time units
5
and
6
(both inclusive),
following the clock
C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat2}}}}
.
The clock
C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat2}}}}
is not reset at the end of this operation.
In
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
we design the
Send
type for sending messages,
this type includes various parameters to describe the aforementioned requirements,
represented as
Send
<[
parameter1
],

[
parameter2
],

...>
.

Assuming that the
Close
(payload) type is defined,
to send it using the
Send
type,
we would begin by writing
Send
<
Close
,

...>
.
If we abbreviate
C_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat2}}}}
as
’
b
’
,
we would use the clock
’
b
’
for the time constraints and
write
Send
<
Close
,

’
b
’,

...>
.
The parameters for the time constraints in the
Send
type are
specified after declaring the clock.
In this case,
both bounds are integers,
and are included in the time window.
Therefore,
the
Send
type would be parameterised
as
Send
<
Close
,

’
b
’,

0,

true
,

1,

false
,

...>
.
Bounds are integers because
generic type parameters are limited to

u8
,

u16
,

u32
,

u64
,

u128
,

usize
,

i8
,

i16
,

i32
,

i64
,

i128
,

isize
,

char
and
bool

[
42
]
.

Next,
we ensure that the clock
’
b
’
is not reset after triggering
the
send
operation.
This is represented as a whitespace
char
value in the
Send
type:

Send
<
Close
,

’
b
’,

0,

true
,

1,

false
,

’

’,

...>
.
The last parameter,
known as the
continuation
,
describes the operation that follows the sending of the integer.
In this case,
we need to close the connection,
which is done with an
End
type.
The complete implementation of the sending operation would
be
Send
<
Close
,

’
b
’,

0,

true
,

1,

false
,

’

’,

End
>
.

In a mirrored fashion,
the receiving end can be instantiated
as
Recv
<
Close
,

’
b
’,

0,

true
,

1,

false
,

’

’,

End
>
.
It is imperative to highlight that
Send

and
Recv
inherently mirror each other,
underscoring their
dual-compatibility
.

Figs.

2(a)
and
2(b)

help explain how

Send
and
Recv
work on the inside,
giving us a detailed look at their parameters and features.
The generic type parameters preceded by
const
within the

Send
and
Recv
types also act as values.
These represent general type categories that
Rust
,
naturally supports.
This type-value duality makes it easier to check
if they match correctly during compilation,
ensuring compatibility between two communicating parties.

We also define time units in relation to clocks,
a topic we explore in more details in

§​

5.1
.
In
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
each
Send
or
Recv
type has a single time constraint.
We have done this for several reasons,
mainly to avoid making the types too complicated and because,
in most situations,
one time constraint is enough.

However,
it is essential to acknowledge that certain specialised cases
necessitate the accommodation of multiple clocks
within specific time windows to validate a singular operation.
These situations typically arise when the clocks
were not initially synchronised or have shifted in relation to each other.
Such issues can be addressed through various strategies,
such as introducing delays to specific clocks
or expanding the protocol with additional message exchanges
to synchronise the clocks.

F.2
Enforcing Time Constraints

To enforce time constraints,
it is essential to use
reliable clocks and APIs
that can accurately and efficiently instantiate
and compare clocks to time constraints.

Rust
’s standard library provides
the
time

[
45
]
module,
which enables developers to start and compare clocks
to measure the duration between events.
The
time
library employs OS API on all platforms,
and it is important to note that
the time provided by this library
differs from
wall clock time
.
Wall clock time,
which is the time displayed on a physical clock,
remains steady and monotonic,
where consecutive events have consecutive time stamps in the same order,
and each second has the same length.

However,
the
time
library has two types of clocks:
the
Instant
type, which is monotonic but non-steady,
and the
SystemTime
type, which is steady but non-monotonic.
It is not possible to have a clock that is both steady and monotonic
due to technical reasons associated with the clock device,
virtualisation,
or OS API bugs.
Nonetheless,
this library provides ways to check
and partially handle
such situations if they arise.
In
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
we use the
Instant
type
since we prioritise the correct order of events.

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ employs the
time
library in two key ways
for the management of virtual clocks.
Firstly,
it uses a dictionary (or
HashMap
in
Rust
)
wherein each key corresponds to a character representing a clock’s name,
and the associated value is of type
Instant
.
When handling message transmissions or receptions,
we compare the relevant clocks with their respective time constraints using this dictionary.

To establish an empty
HashMap
of this nature,
we employ the following code:

let

clocks

=

HashMap
::<
char
,

Instant
>::
new
();
.
Subsequently,
a new clock is introduced to the dictionary through the operation

clocks
.
insert
(’
a
’,

Instant
::
now
());
,
where
’
a
’
denotes the clock’s name,

Figure 13
:
Definition of
send
primitive.

Within our
send
and
recv
primitives,
we implement a cascade of conditions as depicted in

Fig.

13
.
Notably,
we begin by verifying the integrity of the left-hand side
of the time window (referred to as the
lower bound
) in

11
.
Subsequently,
we scrutinise the right-hand side of the time window
(referred to as the
upper bound
) in

13
.
Following this,
we attempt to retrieve the clock
clock

from the
HashMap

clocks
in

17

and compare it with the time constraint associated
with the specific communication operation in

19
.
Should
clock
align correctly with the time constraint,
the payload is sent with a timeout barrier using
send_timeout
in

23
.
If the
send_timeout
operation proves successful in

27
,
and if it is determined in

21

that
clock
requires resetting,
it is indeed reset in

29

before ultimately returning the continuation of type
S

in
30
.

As discerned in

19
,
where we gauge time using
clock
.
elapsed
().
as_secs
()
,
we measure time in seconds for the sake of facilitating testing.
However,
it is important to note that this measurement could alternatively be conducted in milliseconds,
microseconds,
or nanoseconds.
Notably,
we have abstained from employing powerful solvers such as
SMT solvers,
as our focus primarily resides in verification of time constraints
for
Rust
(local) types.
In
§​

F.5
,
within
\nu
Scr
T
,
we elucidate that our verification process entails confirming that,
for each clock,
the left-hand side of each time window precedes the right-hand side of the subsequent one.
This ensures,
for the scenarios we aim to address,
that the time constraints are validated at compile time.

Figure 15
:
Primitives for
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
.

Fig.

1(b)
offers an extensive overview
of the timed protocol associated with our running example.

Figs.

14
and
15
,
in turn,
elucidate the implementation of
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}

with
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​.
In this context,

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ uses the
MeshedChannels
type,
encompassing
n+1
parameters,
with
n
representing the count of roles integrated into the protocol.
These parameters include the role’s name
associated with each
MeshedChannels
,
n-1
binary channels facilitating interaction with other roles,
and a stack that governs the sequence of binary channel usage.

The types relevant to
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
are displayed in

Fig.

14
.
Within

14
to
17
,
we specify the binary types for the first branch,

SerToSatData
and
SerToSenData
,
enabling
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
to engage with
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sen}}}}
,
respectively.
To be precise,
the
SerToSatData
type in
14

signifies that
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
,
on this binary channel,
initiates communication by sending
a message labelled
GetData
to
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
,
before receiving the data in a message labelled
Data

from
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
.
Those two operations employ the clock
a
,
and maintain a time window between
5
and
6
second (both inclusive)
for the first operation,
and between
6
and
7
second (both inclusive) for the second operation.
This clock is reset only within the second operation.

Correspondingly,

18
and
20

define the binary types for
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
in the second branch,
and

21
and
23

enable
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
to make the choice.

StackSerData
in
27

delineates the order of operations for the first branch,
wherein
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
interacts twice with
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
using
RoleSat
,
prior to dispatching a choice with
RoleBroadcast
.
Similarly,

29
and
30

define the stacks for the second branch and for the choice.

32
to
38

encapsulate the aforementioned elements,
along with
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
’s name,

NameSer
,
into
MeshedChannels
.
Specifically,

EndpointSer
initiates the protocol,
while the other two represent
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
’s behaviour in each branch.

Fig.

15
illustrates how
the
EndpointSer
type
is employed as an input in the
Rust
function
endpoint_ser
.
Notably,
the output type of this function,
as indicated in
2
,
is
Result
<(),

Error
>
,
exemplifying the use of affineness in
Rust
.
The
endpoint_ser
function will yield either a unit type
()

or an
Error
as output.

Initially,
this function initialises the clock
a
in

8
,
inserting it into a mutable reference of type

HashMap
named
clocks

(
17
).
It is imperative to note that this virtual clock is role-specific and not shared with other roles.
Subsequently,

endpoint_ser
invokes the function
recurs_ser
and returns its output.
In the event of an error occurring at any point within either of these functions,
the functions panic at this point and
the remaining code within the same scope is dropped.

In
28
,
variable
s
of type
EndpointSerData
,
endeavours to send a contentless message labelled
GetData
.
Here,
by ”endeavours”,
we imply that the
send
function will yield
either a value resembling
EndpointSerData
,
excluding the initial
Send
operation,
or an
Error
.
If the clock’s time fails to adhere to the time constraint
represented in the type
SerToSatData

included in the type
EndpointSerData
,
an
Error
is raised.

Both
send
and
recv
functions serve as syntactic sugar for verifying compliance with time constraints.
They achieve this by comparing the relevant clock provided in the type for the time window and,
if necessary,
resetting the clock before transmitting or receiving the message.
The
clocks
input in those functions
comes in addition to the inputs already
required in their counterparts in
our previous version of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​.

In the subsequent line,

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
seeks to receive a message,
and this process repeats until the protocol loops in

30
.
In the second branch,
after sending the
Close
messages in

24
,
the connection is terminated in

25

using the
close
function,
which shares the same output type as
endpoint_ser
.

In
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
asynchrony is managed by employing the asynchronous primitives
of the
Rust
library
crossbeam_channel
.
The channels used in this library operate as
two sides sharing the same memory cell,
where the sender side can only write to the cell,
and the receiver side can only read from it.
Simulating transfer time in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ involves blocking
the receiving side for each operation.
Other asynchronous designs used in different
Rust
libraries,
such as
tokio

[
46
]
,
may involve blocking the sending side or restricting
any side from accessing the memory cell for a specific duration.
With our primitives,
it is feasible to attempt to receive a message before it is sent.
Each
recv
operation will wait until the end of its time window before failing,
assuming no other errors arise.

F.4
Error Handling

The error handling capabilities of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ encompass various errors
that may arise during protocol implementation and execution.
These errors include the misuse of generated types and timeouts.
This demonstrates the versatility of our implementation in verifying
a wide range of communication protocols,
ranging from examples found in literature to more specific instances,
some of which have been studied and implemented in
§​

6
.

For example,
if Lines
28
and
29

in
Fig.

15
are swapped,
the program will fail to compile because it expects a
send
primitive in

28
,
as indicated by the type of
s
.
Another error that can be detected at compile-time is when
a payload with the wrong type is sent.
For instance,
sending a
Data
message instead of
a
GetData

in
28

will result in a compilation error.

Furthermore,

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ is capable of identifying errors at runtime.
If
22
to
30

are replaced with a single
Ok
(())
,
the code will compile successfully.
However,
during runtime,
the other roles will encounter failures as they consider

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
to have failed.
Timeouts are also handled dynamically.
Introducing a 10 seconds delay simulating a time-consuming
and blocking inner task,
using
sleep
(
Duration
::
from_secs
(10))
,
between
Lines
28
and
29

will cause
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
to sleep for 10 seconds.
As a result,
the
recv
operation in
29

will fail due to a violation
of the associated time constraint,
leading
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
to fail and terminate.
Additionally,
failing to instantiate the clock
a
in

8

will raise a runtime error since the time constraints cannot be
evaluated without the necessary clocks.

F.5
Top-Down Approach

Instead of manually writing local types and endpoints in
Rust
,
a task that may prove onerous and susceptible to errors in the long term,
an alternative approach emerges:
implementing the corresponding global type
and subsequently projecting it onto local
Rust
APIs.
This approach,
known as the Top-Down approach
[
21
]
,
is illustrated in
Fig.

1(a)
(right)
through the architectural design of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​.
To streamline this process,
we introduce
\nu
Scr
T
,
an extension of
\nu
Scr

[
50
]
,
with the primary objective of transforming multiparty protocols
expressed in the
Scribble
language
[
49
]
into global types.

⬇

1

global

protocol

RemoteData
(
role

Sen
,

role

Sat
,

role

Ser
){

2

rec

Loop

{

3

choice

at

Ser

{

4

GetData
()

from

Ser

to

Sat

within

[5;6]

using

a

and

resetting

();

5

GetData
()

from

Sat

to

Sen

within

[5;6]

using

b

and

resetting

();

6

Data
()

from

Sen

to

Sat

within

[6;7]

using

b

and

resetting

(
b
);

7

Data
()

from

Sat

to

Ser

within

[6;7]

using

a

and

resetting

(
a
);

8

continue

Loop

9

}

or

{

10

Close
()

from

Ser

to

Sat

within

[5;6]

using

a

and

resetting

();

Close
()

from

Sat

to

[5;6]

Figure 16
:
Global protocol for remote data in
\nu
Scr
T
.

Fig.

16
presents the global protocol
relevant to the Remote data protocol in
\nu
Scr
T
.
The initial interaction within the running example,
in the first branch following the choice,
entails
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Ser}}}}
dispatching a message labelled

GetData
to
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Sat}}}}
within the time interval of
5
to
6
unit,
governed by the clock
a
.
This is implemented in
4
. Notably,
as shown in
Fig.

16
and
compared to the original
\nu
Scr
keywords,
several additional keywords are introduced in
\nu
Scr
T
:

within
,

using

and
and

resetting
.
These keywords are employed to denote,
respectively,
the time window (indicated by two integers enclosed in open or closed brackets),
the clock to be used (specified as a character),
and the clock that requires resetting
(depicted within parentheses,
encompassing either nothing or a character).

It is pertinent to underscore that despite sharing the same name,
the virtual clocks assigned to both roles are distinct entities.
To ensure the accuracy of timed protocols,

\nu
Scr
T
conducts a series of checks.
Firstly,
it verifies the precise representation of all interactions,
encompassing the five aforementioned components.
Furthermore,
it validates that each time constraint demonstrates a strict increase,
signifying that the lower bound of one operation must not coincide with or occur after the upper bound of the subsequent operation involving the same clock.
Moreover,
each clock,
which should not decrement,
must be capable of satisfying every time constraint,
accounting for resets.
Lastly,

\nu
Scr
T
performs standard checks as per the

MPST
protocols
[
50
]

and transforms a timed
Scribble
protocol into
Communicating Timed Automata
[
4
]
.

Extended Details for
§​

6

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ and
MultiCrusty

(a)

(b)

Mesh protocol

:

(b)

Mesh protocol

Figure 18
:
Global protocols for benchmarks.

To compare
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ and
MultiCrusty
,
we choose two different protocols shown in
Figs.

17
and
18
:
the
ring
protocol,
and the
mesh
protocol.
In the former,
the participants pass a
message from one to the other,
and in the latter,
each participant
sends a message
to each other.
In both protocols,
the loop is run 100 times.
To fairly compare
both libraries,
each operation
must happen within
the same time window:
between
0
and
10
seconds.

(a)

mesh
- compilation

(b)

ring
- compilation

(c)

mesh
- runtime

(d)

ring
- runtime

Figure 19
:
Benchmark results.

Fig.

19

displays our benchmark results,
running from 2 roles to 8 roles.
In all graphs,
the red line with stars
is linked to
MultiCrusty
(
AMPST
in the legend),
and the purple line with triangles
is linked to
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ (
ATMP
in the legend).
The
X
-axis corresponds
to the number of roles
in the protocols;
and the
Y
-axis,
to the time needed for the related task.
In all
compilation
graphs,
the
Y
-axis scale
is in seconds (s);
and in all
running time
graphs,
the
Y
-axis scale
is in milliseconds (ms).

Ring Protocol

is a protocol
where a message is sent
from the first role
to the last role through
each role,
exactly once,
then goes the other
way around,
as shown
in
Figs.

17(a)
and
18(a)
.
The results of the
compile-time
benchmarks are shown
in
Fig.

19(b)
,
and for the
runtime
benchmarks are shown
in
Fig.

19(d)
.
At 2 roles,

MultiCrusty
is compiled less than 2%
faster than
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ and
less than 5% faster at 8 roles,
ranging between 19 s and 20.8 s
for
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​.
This overhead
is also visible for
running time,
where
MultiCrusty
is 15%
faster than
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ at 2 roles,
and then 5% faster
at 8 roles.
The overhead
does not seem to increase
as the number of roles
increases:
there is less than
0.5 ms of difference
between
MultiCrusty
and
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ at 6, 7 and 8 roles.

Mesh Protocol

is a protocol
where all roles
send and receive
a message to each other,
as shown
in
Figs.

17(b)
and
18(b)
.
The results of the
compile-time
(resp.
runtime
) benchmarks are shown
in
Fig.

19(a)
(resp.
Fig.

19(c)
).
The curves of the benchmark results
follow a similar trend
as for the
ring
protocol:

MultiCrusty
is compiled faster than

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ by less than 1%
at 2 roles,
and 4% at 8 roles,
and is running faster
by less than 1% at 2 roles
and 15% at 8 roles.
The compilation times
for
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ range between 18.9 s
and 26 s,
and the running times
are between 0.9 ms
and 11.9 ms.
For both graphs,
the gap exponentially increases,
in absolute values,
due to the exponential
number of enforced time constraints:
there are
2*\binom{n}{2}=n*(n-1)

Send
operations,
where
n
is the number of roles.

Results Summary

All graphs show

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ has
overheads compared
to
MultiCrusty
,
which increases
with the number of roles
in absolute values.
This can be explained
by the light additions
we made to
MultiCrusty
for creating
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​:
the
Send
and
Recv

types in
MultiCrusty
have 2 parameters,
resp. for the payload
and the continuation;
and in
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​,
they have 8 parameters,
2 for the payload
and the continuation,
and 6 for the time constraints.
Unlike
MultiCrusty
,

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​’s

send
and
recv
primitives
use a cascade of
if
/
else

to check the time constraints before all.
Those results show that,
with additional key features
for handling asynchronous
and timed communications
compared to
MultiCrusty
,
we were able to maintain
low overheads overall.

G.2
Case Studies

Apart from the protocols sourced from existing scientific literature,
we have included five additional protocols that use the asynchronous and
timed features of
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ extracted from industrial examples.
Besides the Remote Data protocol detailled earlier,
there are three protocol extracted from

[
25
]
and one protocol extracted from

[
44
,
41
]
.
In particular,
the PineTime Heart Rate protocol

[
39
]
,
extracted from
[
25
]

is a protocol used in the industry
to help the PineTime smartwatch retrieve data from
a heart rate sensor
at a specific pace.

We have not compared
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ with the
crossbeam_channel
library
as
[
32
]
has already conducted such a comparison between their library,

MultiCrusty
,
and the
crossbeam_channel
library.
Consequently,
we can infer similar comparison results between
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ and
crossbeam_channel
.
For the
Mesh
protocol,

MultiCrusty
exhibits superior performance compared to the
crossbeam_channel
library
in terms of the number of roles,
indicating that
\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​ would likewise outperform the
crossbeam_channel
library.
However,
for other protocols,
the
crossbeam_channel
library demonstrates better performance than
MultiCrusty
,
and by extension,

\texttt{MultiCrusty}^{T}
​.
